## Page 1


CLASSICALITY FOR HILBERT MODULAR FORMS
YUANYANG JIANG
Abstract. Let F be a totally real number field. We prove that a character of the spherical Hecke
algebra appearing in the completed cohomology of Hilbert modular varieties is modular if the
associated Galois representation of GalF is absolutely irreducible, and de Rham of regular parallel
weights. As an application, we prove some new cases of the Langlands-Clozel-Fontaine-Mazur
conjecture of GL2 over totally real fields.
For the proof, we generalize the method in [Pan26], calculate geometric partial Fontaine operators,
and study the cohomology of the associated Koszul-type partial de Rham complexes. The key step
is the establishment of a locally analytic Jacquet-Langlands transfer, whose proof consists of several
novel ingredients including a comparison of Igusa stacks for different quaternionic Shimura data
constructed by [DHKZ26], and the Grothendieck-Messing theory for locally analytic infinite level
Shimura varieties established in [Jia26a].

1. Introduction
1.1. Classicality. Let F be any number field, and let p be a prime number. We fix an abstract
isomorphism ι : ¯Qp ∼= C. The Langlands-Clozel-Fontaine-Mazur conjecture predicts a bijection
between the set of n-dimensional irreducible geometric representations of GalF and the set of algebraic
cuspidal automorphic representations of GLn(AF ).
Here, we say that a Galois representation
ρ : GalF →GLn(¯Qp) is “geometric” if it is continuous, de Rham at all places above p and unramified
away from finitely many places.
A highly successful approach to this conjecture is by proving a modularity lifting theorem, which
establishes the modularity of ρ under the assumption that its reduction ¯ρ is residually modular.
Based on the previous works on Serre’s conjecture ([Böc01], [DFG04], [KW09a], [KW09b], [Kis09a]),
[Kis09b] and [Eme11] established the Langlands-Clozel-Fontaine-Mazur conjecture for n = 2 and
F = Q with mild generic conditions.
The strategy of [Eme11] consists of two steps:
(1) Assuming that ¯ρ is residually modular, one proves a “promodularity” theorem to realize ρ in
the completed cohomology defined in [Eme06];


## Page 3


CLASSICALITY FOR HILBERT MODULAR FORMS
3
(2) One proves a “classicality” result: for Hecke eigenclasses appearing in the completed cohomology,
the de Rhamness condition implies modularity.
In [Eme11], both steps rely crucially on the p-adic local Langlands correspondence for GL2(Qp),
which is unavailable for other groups. Nevertheless, in the Hilbert case, the remarkable work of
[BHHMS23], combined with the ideas of [GN22], establishes the step (1) under a generic condition.
Our work focuses on the step (2), namely classicality, in the Hilbert setting. Throughout the
article, we fix F to be a totally real number field of dimension d. Let L ⊂¯Qp be a finite extension
of Qp containing all embeddings of F, with ring of integer OL and residue field F. For any place ℓof
Q, let Σℓdenote the set of places of F over ℓ.
Let G := ResF/Q(GL2). For any neat open compact subgroup K ⊂G(Af), we have the d-
dimensional Hilbert modular variety ShK(G) of level K defined over Q, which is smooth, quasi-
projective, and of abelian type (see §2.2).
Following [Eme06], we fix a neat open compact subgroup Kp ⊂G(Ap
f) and define the i-th

where the colimit is taken over all open compact subgroups Kp ⊂G(Qp).
Let S be a fixed finite set of places of Q, including p and ∞, as well as all places that ramify in F
or Kp. Denote by TS the spherical Hecke algebra over Zp generated by the spherical Hecke operators
of G at finite places away from S. Then ˜Hi(Kp, L) is equipped with an action of TS ×G(Qp)×GalQ.
The following classicality theorem is the main result of this paper.
Theorem 1.1.1 (Theorem 5.1.3). Let ρ : GalF,S →GL2(L) be a continuous representation. Let
χ : TS →L be the associated character determined by the Eichler-Shimura relation (31).
We assume that
(1) ˜Hd(Kp, L)[χ] := HomTS(χ, ˜Hd(Kp, L)) ̸= 0;
(2) ρ is absolutely irreducible;
(3) ρ is de Rham of regular parallel weights.
Then the character χ is classical, i.e. there exists a Hilbert modular eigenform f, such that TS
acts on f via χ. In other words, ρ ∼= ρf.
Our proof differs from that of [Eme11], and builds upon the idea of [Pan26]. In particular, we do
not use any p-adic local Langlands correspondence.
Remark 1.1.2. After the pioneering work of Lue Pan ([Pan26]) on modular curves, there have been
several generalizations: [QS25] proves a version for τ-locally analytic completed cohomology of
Shimura curves, and [Mat25] proves a version for the partially completed cohomology of unitary
Shimura surfaces assuming that Fp ∈{Qp, Qp2} for all p ∈Σp.
Our work requires no assumptions on p and F, and p may even be ramified. Furthermore, we work
with the full locally analytic completed cohomology, allowing our method to potentially describe the
internal structures of the (conjectural) locally analytic representations arising from the p-adic local
Langlands correspondence (see Remark 1.2.6).
Remark 1.1.3. The technical condition of being of parallel weights is a consequence of current
limitations in understanding “geometric Fontaine operators”. I hope to remove this condition in the
future.
As an application, combining our work with [BHHMS23] yields new cases of the Langlands-Clozel-
Fontaine-Mazur conjecture:

Theorem 1.1.4 (Theorem 5.4.4). Assume that p is inert in F. Let ρ : GalF,S →GL2(L) be a
continuous representation. Let ¯ρ : GalF →GL2(F) be the reduction of ρ.
Assume that
(1) ¯ρ is residually modular.
(2) ¯ρ|GalF ( p√
1) is absolutely irreducible.
(3) ¯ρ|GalFp is semisimple generic in the sense of [BHHMS23, p.100 (i)].
(4) For any w ∈S\{p}, the framed deformation ring of ¯ρ|GalFw over W(F) is formally smooth.
(5) ρ|GalFp is de Rham of regular parallel weights {k, 1 −k} for some k ∈Z.
Then there exists a Hilbert modular eigenform f such that ρ ∼= ρf.
This theorem follows from the classicality theorem (Theorem 1.1.1), the results of [BHHMS23],
and an argument utilizing the ℓ-adic Hecke action of [FS21] (Corollary 2.7.12).
Remark 1.1.5. This theorem leaves significant room for improvement. The technical conditions are
imposed to apply the theorem of [BHHMS23] directly. For example, p being unramified rather than
being inert should suffice, and in (5), we could consider general regular parallel weights rather than
the special cases {k, 1 −k}.
1.2. Strategy. For this introduction, let us focus on the classicality theorem (Theorem 1.1.1). Our
proof follows closely the idea of [Pan26] to understand the de Rhamness condition.
By [Fon04], we know that for a finite dimensional Hodge-Tate representation ρ, the de Rhamness
is determined by the vanishing of a certain Fontaine operator acting on DpdR(ρ), where the subscript
refers to “presque de Rham”. See §4.1 for details.
In [Pan26], Pan observes that the analogue of Fontaine operators exists for the infinite dimensional
Galois representation given by the locally analytic completed cohomology, and that it arises from a
certain geometric Fontaine operator, defined by concrete differential operators on the infinite level
Shimura varieties. Thus the de Rhamness condition can be understood in terms of the kernel of
these differential operators, i.e. the cohomolgy of the associated D-modules.
The construction of these differential operators has been generalized to arbitrary Shimura varieties
in [Jia26a], and the associated D-modules is precisely the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine
complex introduced in [Jia26a, Definition 3.8.18].
Thus we have the following general proof strategy towards classicality:
(1) Given an irreducible ρ with the associated character χρ : TS →L, we want to relate ρ
with the infinite-dimensional Galois representation ˜Hd(Kp, L)la[χρ], where (−)la denotes the
subspace of G(Qp)-locally analytic vectors.
(2) We construct Fontaine operators for ˜H∗(Kp, L)la, and relate them to the differential operators
constructed in [Jia26a].
(3) We study the automorphic property of the cohomology of the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-
Fontaine complexes, and try to answer [Jia26a, Question 3.8.21].
In what follows, we will explain how we resolve each step in the Hilbert case, with more emphasis on
the step (3).
1.2.1. Plectic Conjecture. For modular curves, the step (1) follows immediately from the Eichler-
Shimura relation:
˜H1(Kp, L)la[χρ] ∼= ρ ⊗Πla(ρχ)
where Πla(ρχ) is a locally analytic representation of G(Qp). However, the general situation is more
delicate.

Now Theorem 1.2.4 follows from the following stronger theorem:
Theorem 1.2.5 (Theorem 3.2.6). The complexes Fib(dτ, ¯dσ), Fib(dτ, dσ), Fib( ¯dτ, ¯dσ), Fib( ¯dτ, dσ)
have classical cohomology groups. Here being “classical” is in the same sense as in Theorem 1.2.4.
This is the deepest result of this paper. The treatments of Fib( ¯dτ, ¯dσ) and Fib(dτ, dσ) are similar
to those in [Pan26]: the cohomology of Fib( ¯dτ, ¯dσ) ∼= Osm is the colimit of coherent cohomology of

Kp. However, the classicality of the rigid

## Page 8


8
YUANYANG JIANG
The more mysterious is Fib( ¯dτ, dσ), which is some sort of “partial de Rham complex”. Our strategy
is to find a suitable stratification, and define a locally analytic Jacquet-Langlands correspondence
(Theorem 1.2.11 (4)) relating this partial de Rham cohomology to coherent cohomology of quaternionic
Shimura curves. We will make some preparations on analytic stacks before stating Theorem 1.2.11.
Remark 1.2.6. As in [Pan26], the bi-filtration (2) provides evidence for the expected internal
structure of the locally analytic representations of GL2(Fp) in the conjectural p-adic local Langlands
correspondence, as predicted by [BH15], [BHHMS24], [Din24]. It should be related to the square
appearing in [Din24, Conjecture 1.7] via translation functors. In fact, one can calculate the wall-
crossing of the graded pieces, following the argument in [Su25].
1.2.4. Analytic stacks. Although Theorem 1.2.5 can be stated in terms of sheaves on analytic site of
Stor
Kp(G), it is difficult to manipulate these sheaves involving differential operators within the classical
framework. For one thing, the perfectoid geometry automatically ignores differential information,
and for another thing, there does not exist a general six functor formalism for sheaves on analytic
sites to the best of our knowledge. Furthermore, we need to take into account the topological
properties of these sheaves. The theory of analytic stacks of Clausen-Scholze provides a satisfying
solution to all these problems.
In [Jia26a], we constructed various realizations of infinite-level Shimura varieties as analytic stacks.
Let us recall some constructions here. Readers are referred to the introduction of [Jia26a] for details.
Roughly, we define the locally analytic Hilbert modular variety SKp(G)la ([Jia26a, Definition
3.3.16]) as the relative (analytic) spectrum of the sheaf Ola, which is a refinement of the perfectoid
Hilbert modular variety SKp(G) and could be thought of as a ringed space (|SKp(G)|, Ola). Note
that in [Jia26a], Shimura varieties were denoted by curly X such as X la
Kp, and we are sorry for
changing the notation here.
On the other hand, we want an enhancement of the topological space |SKp(G)|. For this, we
consider the theory of analytic de Rham stacks of [Rod24] and [ABLRS25], which is an ideal
framework for studying differential operators. We denote by SKp(G)dR the analytic de Rham stack
of SKp(G), and then it follows by construction that the partial de Rham complexes in Theorem
1.2.5 can be naturally enhanced to quasicoherent sheaves on SKp(G)dR.
The four constructions fit into the following sequence
SKp(G) →SKp(G)la →SKp(G)dR →|SKp(G)|.
The partial de Rham complexes arises from the interplay between SKp(G)la and SKp(G)dR. In

Theorem 1.2.11 (4) shows that the Jacquet-Langlands transfer changes partial de Rham cohomology
over Hilbert modular surfaces into coherent cohomology over quaternionic curves. This type of
phenomena was first observed in [Pan26, Theorem 5.3.20]. This is the crucial technical ingredient
for the proof of Theorem 1.2.5 in §3.4. See §1.2.6 for a sketch of the argument.
Let us say a few words about the proof of Theorem 1.2.11. Using Remark 1.2.8, the part (4)
follows immediately from the part (3) via an explicit calculation along JLper,dR. The part (1) follows
from the parts (2) and (3) by Theorem 1.2.7.
For the parts (2) and (3), we will first prove the following perfectoid version of Jacquet-Langlands
correspondence, which is of independent interest:
Theorem 1.2.12 (Theorem 2.8.9). There is a canonical isomorphism of perfectoid spaces over Cp

The proof of Theorem 1.2.12 is strongly motivated by [TX16], where a similar result is proven for
special fibers of different unitary Shimura varieties by constructing explicit p-quasi isogenies. In this
article, using the Igusa stacks introduced by [Zha23] and further developed by [DHKZ24], [Kim25],
[DHKZ26], we give a more streamlined proof. Roughly, the Igusa stack parametrizes abelian schemes
(with PEL structures) up to p-quasi isogenies, and we establish Theorem 1.2.12 by constructing an
open immersion from the Igusa stack of SKp(G{σ}) to that of SKp(G∅).
Given Theorem 1.2.12, the part (2) of Theorem 1.2.11 follows immediately from the properties of
the functor of taking analytic de Rham stacks established in [ABLRS25]. The proof of Theorem 1.2.11
(3) is more involved. It is easy to construct a perfectoid version of the commutative diagram, but as
an extra ingredient, one needs to compare the de Rham Gc-torsors on SKp(G∅)dR and SKp(G{σ})dR,
which encodes information about connections. The proof of this theorem uses [Jia26a, Corollary
3.10.7], which is a reformulation of the logarithmic Riemann-Hilbert correspondence of [LZ17] and
[DLLZ22].
Remark 1.2.13. The theorems in the main text are more general, relating the I-basic locus of the
(d−|T|)-dimensional quaternionic Shimura varieties SKp(GT ) (Definition 2.8.4) to the (d−|T|−|I|)-
dimensional quaternionic Shimura varieties SKp(GT ` I). If T ` I = Σ∞, this specializes to the

locus for varying I ⊂Σ∞, I ̸= ∅. This is the key to the inductive proof of Theorem 1.2.5 in §3.4.

1.3. Organization of the paper. The paper is organized as follows:
In Section 2, we review the general theory of Igusa stacks following [Zha23], [DHKZ24], [Kim25],
[DHKZ26]. Then we define the quaternionic Shimura varieties, and the related unitary Shimura
varieties, we will construct their Igusa stacks, and show that the Igusa stacks for different quaternionic
Shimura varieties can be embedded into that for the Hilbert modular varieties. This implies Theorem
1.2.12. We also give another application to ℓ-adic cohomology, which implies in particular that the
completed cohomolgy of certain lower dimensional quaternionic Shimura varieties can be realized as
a direct summand of that of higher dimensional ones.
In Section 3, we combine the results in [Jia26a] and Section 2 to prove the locally analytic Jacquet-
Langlands transfer between different quaternionic Shimura varieties (Theorem 3.3.13, Corollary
3.3.11), using which we give an inductive proof of the classicality theorem for partial de Rham
cohomology (Theorem 3.2.6).
In Section 4, we define the Fontaine operators, and prove Theorem 1.2.2. Then we define the
Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex to be the Koszul complex defined by the Fontaine
operators, and show it has classical cohomolgy using Theorem 3.2.6.
In Section 5, we state the theorem on the plectic Lie algebra action (Theorem 5.2.8) in a joint
work with Lue Pan, and combine the ingredients to finish the proof of Theorem 1.1.1.


## Page 13

13
In Appendix A, we will define the p-adic Hecke algebra ˆTS acting on completed cohomology
of Hilbert modular varieties, and define a 2-dimensional determinant of GalF valued in ˆTS in the
sense of [Che14]. The result should be well-known, but we give an account here due to the lack of
references.

2. Perfectoid Jacquet-Langlands correspondence
In this section, we will prove a product formula (Theorem 2.8.9), which relates the geometry of
different quaternionic Shimura varieties. We first learn a result of this type in [TX16]. The result
loc. cit. is concerned with the special fibers, while our result is about the rigid generic fibers. In


## Page 14


14
YUANYANG JIANG
particular, our results are not conditional on the unramifiedness of p in F, and our results hold on
the infinite level at p.
Roughly, quaternionic Shimura varieties are related to certain moduli problems of abelian schemes
with PEL structures, and different choices of the quaternion algebras give rise to different Kottwitz
signature conditions. The proof strategy of [TX16] consists of constructing morphisms between
different quaternionic Shimura varieties by constructing explicit p-quasi-isogenies to alter the signa-
tures. Nowadays, this procedure can be streamlined using the Igusa stacks, constructed in [Zha23],
[DHKZ24], [Kim25], [DHKZ26], which are vaguely moduli problems of abelian schemes with PEL
structures up to p-quasi-isogenies.
Hence, our proof strategy is to show that all the relevant quaternionic Shimura varieties have the
same Igusa stack; more precisely, we will show that the Igusa stacks of various quaternionic Shimura
varieties admit open immersions into the Igusa stack of Hilbert modular varieties.
This fact is well expected by experts, and it could be proven using the relation of the Igusa stacks
with the canonical models shown in [DHKZ24], and by constructing certain “exotic correspondences”
over the canonical models. Examples of such results are in [XZ17].
Our proof is more elementary. In fact, our method is based on the following simple observation:
for Shimura varieties of PEL type, whose Igusa stacks are explicitly constructed in [Zha23], the
Kottwitz condition does not play a role in the definition, since it is already remembered by the part
of FlG,µ in the Zhang’s Cartesian diagram. Thus we give a modified definition of the Igusa stack
by simply forgetting the Kottwitz signature condition, and then we show that the desired Zhang’s
Cartesian diagram still holds.
Apart from an application to the product formula (Theorem 2.8.9), the identification of the Igusa
stacks also allow additional flexibility in relating ℓ-adic cohomology of different quaternionic Shimura
varieties. We will show in §2.7 how to realize the completed cohomolgy of lower dimensional Shimura
varieties as a direct summand as that of higher dimensional Shimura varieties, which should be of
some independent interest. This result will be used later for proving promodularity.
The section is structured as follows: in §2.1, we introduce the general notion of Igusa stacks
following [Kim25]. In §2.2, we introduce the Shimura data that define the quaternionic Shimura
varieties and the unitary Shimura varieties. In §2.3 we define the “good reduction locus", over
which we are going to define the reduction map to the Igusa stack. In §2.4, we construct an Igusa
stack of PEL type and prove a Cartesian diagram in our setting. We follow the original idea of
[Zha23] with a slight modification, such that the comparison of the resulting Igusa stack between
different Shimura varieties becomes obvious (see Remark 2.4.1). We then construct Igusa stacks
for the unitary Shimura varieties in §2.5, and for the quaternionic Shimura varieties in §2.6. As
an application, we show in §2.7 a version of ℓ-adic Jacquet-Langlands correspondence, relating
the completed cohomology of different quaternionic Shimura varieties (Corollary 2.7.12). In §2.8,
via analyzing the local data, we show a version of the product formula (Theorem 2.8.9) involving
different quaternionic Shimura varieties and basic local Shimura varieties.

unless T = ∅. So we have finished the proof in the case where T ̸= ∅.

2.4. Igusa stack of PEL type. We define an Igusa stack for ˜S◦
Up(G′
˜T ) in this subsection. Our
definition is a mild modification of those in [Zha23]. A version in the case of general Shimura varieties
of Hodge type has been obtained in [DHKZ24].
We insist on giving an independent construction here, because we will need a comparison of Igusa
stacks associated to different Shimura varieties. Note that [DHKZ24] uses crucially the theory of
integral models, and it is possible to prove the same comparison using exotic correspondence as in
[XZ17], but the results there seem to require some technical conditions.
Remark 2.4.1 (Difference with [Zha23]). Our definition of Igusa stacks will be slightly different
from the previous work. More precisely, we remove the Kottwitz signature condition in [Zha23,
Definition 8.1]. In this way, the comparison of Igusa stacks becomes only a matter of definition. The
key observation is that the Kottwitz signature condition is remembered by the flag variety and is
forgotten by the Igusa stack.

Dieudonné module of ¯A[p∞]. Thus we have a p-quasi-isogeny
¯A[p∞] ∼= G ×R+ R+/ϖ.
By Serre-Tate theory, we can find a unique abelian scheme A (up to p-quasi-isogeny) over R+
such that A[p∞] is p-quasi-isogeny to G, and A admits a p-quasi-isogeny A ×R+ R+/ϖ ∼= ¯A. By
functoriality, we have an action ι of ODT on A, which coincides with ¯ι after reduction. Similarly, we
have a polarization induced by the symplectic structure on Λ ˜T and on ¯A. Note that the induced
map f : A →A∨over R#,+ satisfies that f = f∨and that f/ϖ : ¯A →¯A∨is a polarization over
R#,+/ϖ, so f is also a polarization.
Finally, we note that we can identify Λ ˜T [1/p] with TpA[1/p], and Λ ˜T ⊗R# ↠L can be identified
with the Hodge-Tate sequence TpA ⊗R# ↠Lie(A). This follows from the same proof of [SW20,
Theorem 14.1.1]. In particular, we can choose the representative in the p-isogeny class such that we
have a fixed isomorphism TpA ∼= Λ ˜T . It also follows that the desired Kottwitz signature condition
holds. The U p-level structure on ¯A also induces naturally a U p-level structure on ¯A.
The construction is clearly functorial.
□

the construction is natural in Λ and in Kp.

that τ, σ ∈Σ∞/p. We can then conclude by Corollary 2.7.11.

Using either ¯d or d, we can define a “de Rham complex”, i.e. a Koszul complex. By studying ¯d,
we will see that the Koszul complex that it defines concentrates in degree 0, and is isomorphic to
Osm
Kp. Hence its cohomology is the direct limit of the coherent cohomology of Shimura varieties on
the finite level.

## Page 41


CLASSICALITY FOR HILBERT MODULAR FORMS
41
As mentioned above, if (I, J) = (∅, Σ∞), then the cohomology of dRI,J(Ola,0
Kp ) is classical in the
sense that the cohomology groups as Hecke modules are supported at points that correspond to
Hilbert modular forms. The main theorem of this section (Theorem 3.2.6) shows that the same
“classicality” holds whenever I ∪J = Σ∞. In Section 4, we will see that the “Bernstein-Gelfand-
Gelfand-Fontaine complex” is filtered by such dRI,J(Ola,0
Kp ) (or its variant with non-trivial coefficient),
and we will use Theorem 3.2.6 to show that the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex has
classical cohomology (Theorem 4.6.2), which in turn will be used in Section 5 to show Theorem 1.1.1.
Note that such classicality is proven in [Pan26] in the case of modular curves. To treat the case
where (I, J) = (Σ∞, ∅), the idea loc. cit. is to consider the Newton stratification, and over each
Newton stratum, the complex dRΣ∞,∅(Ola,0
Kp ) is related to the rigid cohomology of the associated
rigid variety. However, the cases when neither I nor J is an empty set need significantly new ideas.
The idea of our proof of Theorem 3.2.6 is to use induction. For this purpose, we actually prove the
theorem not only for Hilbert modular varieties, but also for all the quaternionic Shimura varieties.
The cohomology of the “partial de Rham complex” in our setting is a mixture of coherent
cohomology and rigid cohomology. In particular, in the extreme case where J = Σ∞, the desired
classicality is obvious by the argument above. In the general case, we use the product formula
(Theorem 2.8.9), which somehow translates the operator d on one Shimura variety to the operator ¯d
on another Shimura variety of lower dimension, which we will refer to as locally analytic Jacquet-
Langlands transfer (Theorem 3.3.13). This idea already appears in [Pan26].
However, in our setting, the proof becomes more complicated because Theorem 2.8.9 is proven
using the perfectoid method. The new ingredients of our proof is provided by the results of [Jia26a],
notably Theorem 3.4.6 and Corollary 3.10.7 loc. cit., which show that the information about
connections can indeed be recovered from the perfectoid data.
The section is structured as follows: we set up the notation in §3.1. In §3.2, using the results from
[Jia26a] (especially Corollary 3.8.9 loc. cit.), we construct the differential operators (Definition 3.2.2),
define the “partial de Rham complexes” (Definition 3.2.4) and state the main theorem (Theorem 3.2.6).
In §3.3, based on Theorem 2.8.9, we prove the locally analytic Jacquet-Langlands correspondence
(Corollary 3.3.11 and Theorem 3.3.13). Finally, we conclude the proof of the main theorem (Theorem
3.2.6) in §3.4.

Definition 3.2.5 (Classical Hecke modules). Let C be a D(¯Q)-linear stable ∞-category, TS
Q :=
TS ⊗Z Q, let M ∈ModTS
Q(C ), and let (k, w) be a regular multiweight. Let S(M(k,w)) be the
set of characters χf : TS
Q →¯Q that correspond to classical Hilbert modular TS-eigenforms f of
weight (max(2 −k, k), w) that are unramified away from the finite set S, with max(2 −k, k) :=
(max(2 −kτ, kτ))τ∈Σ∞.
Then we say that M ∈ModTS
Q(C ) is classical of weight (k, w) as a Hecke module, if for any prime
ideal a ⊂TS¯Q, if a ̸= Ker(χf) for any χf ∈S(M(k,w)), then Ma ∼= 0.
The main theorem of this section is the following:

Remark 3.4.5. Since the proof will be a bit long, let us first give a sketch of the proof:
(1) The case for (I, J) follows easily from the case for (I, J\I);
(2) For any I ` J = T c, and for any non-empty I0 ⊂I, we apply the locally analytic Jacquet-
Langlands transfer (Theorem 3.3.13) and the inductive hypothesis to conclude the classicality of
dRI,J over the I0-basic locus;
(3) The union of all the I0-basic loci for non-empty I0 ⊂I covers everything but a cetain
“I-ordinary locus”, over which we can relate dRI,J directly to dRI,T c;
(4) To show the classicality of dRI,T c over the I-ordinary locus, we reverse the procedure, and
use the classicality over the I0-basic locus, as well as the classicality of the global section of dRI,T c
(Example 3.2.9).

4. Fontaine operator
In this section, we define the geometric Fontaine operators, and relate them to the differential
operators d−kτ+1
τ
and ¯d−kτ+1
τ
that we have defined over Stor(GT )dR (Theorem 4.3.6).
In §4.1, we will give a general discussion of Fontaine operators.
In §4.2, we compute the graded pieces of Bla
dR after fixing infinitesimal character.
In §4.3, we define the geometric Fontaine operators, and state the main theorem relating the
geometric Fontaine operators to differential operators introduced in §3.2 (Theorem 4.3.6). The proof
of Theorem 4.3.6 is quite long and technical, and will be finished in §4.4 and §4.5. The key idea is to
explore the extra structure (see Proposition 4.4.2 for details), which then pins down the operator up
to a scalar. One key ingredient of the argument is the work of [GL21].
Finally in §4.6, we combine all the Fontaine operators into a complex, which we refer to Bernstein-
Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex. We will establish the classicality of the BGGF complex utilizing
Theorems 3.2.6 and 4.3.6. This will be the only output needed for proving the classicality theorem
for Hilbert modular forms (Theorem 5.1.3).
Remark 4.0.1. Before we start, let us remark that with Theorem 3.2.6 established, we will not use
the formalism of analytic stacks from now on. Instead, we will work on the analytic site of Stor
Kp(GT ).
The translation is done as follows: we have natural maps

on the analytic site of Stor

Lemma 4.1.5 ([Fon04, Théorème 4.1]). If V is a continuous Hodge-Tate representation of GalK
over Qp of finite dimension, then V is de Rham if and only if the action of Fontaine operator ∇K
on Darith(V ⊗Qp BdR) is zero.
Proof. Note that Darith(−) coincides with DpdR(−) loc. cit., and the statement is the content of
[Fon04, Théorème 4.1]. Let us give a proof here. By Sen theory, dimK(ζp∞)((V ⊗Cp)hH,R−ΓK−la) =
dimQp(V ), and by the argument in Notation 4.1.4,
dimK(ζp∞)(Darith(V ⊗BdR)) = dimK(ζp∞)((V ⊗Cp)hH,R−ΓK−la) = dimQp(V ).
Note that
DdR(V ) ∼= (V ⊗BdR)GalK ∼= ((V ⊗BdR)hH,R−ΓK−la)ΓK ∼= Darith(V ⊗BdR)ΓK,
where ΓK acts on Darith(V ⊗BdR) locally analytically, and the element ∇K in the Lie algebra acts
nilpotently. In particular, we have an injection
DdR(V ) ⊗K K(ζp∞) ,→Darith(V ⊗BdR),
where ∇K acts on the LHS trivially. If V is de Rham, then by comparing the dimension, this
injection is forced to be an isomorphism, and thus ∇K acts on the RHS also trivially. On the other
hand, if ∇K acts on Darith(V ⊗BdR) trivially, then the action of ΓK is smooth, and then by Galois
descent
dimK(DdR(V )) = dimK(Darith(V ⊗BdR)ΓK) = dimK(ζp∞)(Darith(V ⊗BdR)) = dimQp(V )
and V is de Rham.
□

5. Application to classicality
In this section, as a global application, we show a classicality theorem for Hecke eigenclasses in
the completed cohomology of Hilbert modular varieties (Theorem 5.1.3). As a corollary, we also
prove some cases of the Langlands-Clozel-Fontaine-Mazur conjecture for GL2 over totally real fields
(Theorem 5.4.4). In fact, it is not difficult to also prove some classicality results for other quaternionic
Shimura varieties using Theorem 4.6.2, which we omit here for brevity.
5.1. Classicality theorem. For this section, we fix G := ResF/Q(GL2), and we consider the tower
of Hilbert modular varieties ShK(G) as in §2.2 for T = ∅. Let d := [F : Q]. We fix a neat tame level
Kp ⊂G(Ap
f). Let S be a finite set of places of Q, including p and ∞and all the ramified places
of Kp or F. Denote by TS the spherical Hecke algebra over Zp generated by the spherical Hecke
operators of G at finite places away from S.
Definition 5.1.1. We define the completed cohomology (resp. with compact support) defined by
[Eme06] for the tower of the Shimura varieties {SKpKp(G)} as
RΓ(Kp, L) := Rlim

that is, χ(Sl) := Nm(l)−1 det(ρ(Frl)) and χ(Tl) := Tr(Frl).
Theorem 5.1.3. Let ρ : GalF,S →GL2(L) be a continuous representation, with the associated
character χ determined by the Eichler-Shimura relation (31).
We assume that
(1) ˜Hd(Kp, L)[χ] := HomTS(χ, ˜Hd(Kp, L)) ̸= 0;
(2) ρ is absolutely irreducible;
(3) There exist k ∈Z≤0, w ∈Z, k ≡w (mod 2), such that for each τ ∈Σ∞, ρ is τ-de Rham, and
has τ-Hodge-Tate weights k−w

.
Then the character χ is classical, i.e. there exists a Hilbert modular eigenform f of parallel weight
(2 −k, w), such that TS acts on f via χ. In other words, ρ ∼= ρf.
Let us sketch here the idea of the proof of Theorem 5.1.3, which follows closely the idea of
[Pan26].
The key ingredient is Theorem 4.6.2.
To apply the theorem, we need to show that
the de Rhamness condition will force the Hecke eigenvalue to appear in the cohomology of the
Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex.
There are some subtleties in the implementation of the strategy:
(1) Before we can reach the Bernstein-Gelfand-Gelfand-Fontaine complex, we need to determine
the infinitesimal character of the Hecke eigenspace.
(2) We expect to see the tensor induction of the Galois representation rather than the Galois
representation itself in the completed cohomology. Hence an additional semisimplicity result is
required in the case where ⊗-IndQ
F ρ is reducible.
Both (1) and (2) (in the strongly irreducible case) will be solved using the plectic action constructed
in a joint work with Lue Pan ([JP26]). We will summarize the relevant result in §5.2. We finish the
proof of the main theorem (Theorem 5.1.3) in §5.3.
Remark 5.1.4. For (1), a result about infinitesimal character has been obtained by [DPS25] under
weakly non-Eisenstein assumption. For (2), the semisimplicity could also be proven by adapting the
argument of [Nek18]. On the other hand, our method in [JP26] has no assumption on the reduction
¯ρ, and establishes an isomorphism
˜Hd(Kp, L)la[χ] ∼= ⊗-IndQ
F ρ ⊗Πla(ρ)
for some locally analytic representation Πla(ρ).

Conjecturally, the statement should hold assuming only the absolute irreducibility of ρ rather than
the strong irreducibility. However, the strong irreducibility assumption does not cause any trouble
for our proof of classicality, thanks to the following dichotomy between the strongly irreducible case
and the CM case:
Lemma 5.2.9. Let ρ : GalF,S →GL2(L) be an absolutely irreducible continuous Galois representa-
tion. Assume that there exists τ such that the τ-Hodge-Tate weights are distinct. Then
• either ρ is strongly irreducible;
• or ρ has multiplication, that is, there exists a quadratic extension E/F that is unramified
away from S, and a continuous character η : GalE,S →GL1(L), such that ρ ∼= IndGalE,S
GalF,S (η).

If in addition ρ is de Rham of regular τ-Hodge-Tate weights for all τ, then in the second case, E has
to be a CM extension, that is, ρ has complex multiplication.
Remark 5.2.10. By class field theory, for fixed S, there are only finitely many such quadratic
extensions.
Proof. Assume that ρ is not strongly irreducible. Then there exists an open normal subgroup
U ⊂GalF,S, such that ρ|U is reducible. Consider the Lie algebra Lie(ρ(U)) ⊂gl2,L. Since ρ
is reducible as a representation of Lie(ρ(U)), Lie(ρ(U)) must be contained in a Borel, which we
conjugate (up to extending L) to be the sub-Lie algebra of upper-triangular matrices bL ⊂gl2,L.
We claim that Lie(ρ(U)) actually factors through the torus; otherwise, the normalizer of Lie(ρ(U))
in GL2(L) will also be contained in the subgroup of upper-triangular matrices, and thus ρ cannot be
irreducible. Since there exists τ such that the τ-Hodge-Tate weights are regular, by [Sen73], we know
that the map from Lie(ρ(U′)) to the maximal torus of Lie(PGL2) is surjective. Therefore, ρ(GalF,S)
is contained in the normalizer of the Cartan subalgebra of sl2,L, that is,
ρ(GalF,S) ⊂T(L) ∪w0T(L),
where T is the maximal torus of GL2 of diagonal matrices, and w0 :=

Since ρ is irreducible, U′ ̸= GalF,S. So U′ is an open subgroup of index 2, and thus corresponds to
a quadratic extension E/F unramified away from S. The restriction ρ|U′ decomposes as η1 ⊕η2, for
ηi : U′ = GalE,S →GL1(L), and by adjunction, we have a non-zero map IndGalF,S
GalE,S(η1) →ρ, which
has to be an isomorphism since ρ is irreducible.

Appendix A. Universal determinants on Hilbert modular varieties
